#7424. WTF is a unified media platform?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Editor’s note: These technologies are ill-defined, please share feedback to “editorial@digiday.com” to talk more.

Ad tech has never been short on acronyms. Its latest crop includes: unified advertising platforms (UAPs), unified media platforms (UMPs), and unified buying platforms (UBPs).

This kind of language has added a new layer of confusion to the space, and ad executives across adland have been saying, “WTF…?” Digiday is here to help by trying to bring some clarity to the matter.

A simple way to approach the question is to think of such terms — UAP, UBP and UMP — as overlapping labels for technology that promises to make advertising easier to buy, manage and optimize across an increasingly fragmented media landscape.

Of course, AI is central to the pitch. Advocates of such technologies maintain that instead of separately operating campaigns across Google, Meta, Amazon and the open web, advertisers could potentially use one system to determine where money goes, then execute those decisions through the relevant platforms.

Some providers propose bringing buying and selling infrastructure together. Others want to sit above existing systems and coordinate them. This is an important distinction to keep in mind, as connective software is not the same as controlling how an advertiser spends its next dollar.

At its simplest, a UMP coordinates planning, buying, optimization, and measurement across multiple media channels. Its remit could include programmatic display, CTV, retail media, search, social, and direct publisher relationships.

According to most sources, the idea is not necessarily to replace every existing media trading infrastructure, i.e., demand-side and supply-side platforms, etc., but to make them work together more efficiently.

That is distinct from the traditional remit of a DSP, many of which have spent years expanding the inventory and formats available through their own buying interfaces. A unified platform, by contrast, promises to operate across buying environments that no single DSP controls.

Jay Friedman, co-founder of CartographAI, told Digiday that the emerging proposition is about budget fluidity, not simply consolidating DSPs and SSPs. “We define a UMP as a platform that executes buys across all primary digital channels (search, social, programmatic, and direct). This may or may not include agents,” Friedman told Digiday. “Traditionally, this has been the domain of agency order management systems,” he said, noting how several new entrants are hoping to challenge these players.

The terms overlap, but they differ in what they promise to unify. The chart below traces that range, from handling an individual transaction to coordinating campaigns across channels and deciding where budgets go.

Keith Weisberg of Unplugged Collective told Digiday UBPs would initially come to market as a repackaging of existing tools. He said the deeper shift would come when buyers could bring their data to the places ads run and delegate spending decisions to AI agents.

“I’d treat that as a net-new product, not a DSP feature,” he said. “However, we’re all plugged into DSPs, and even if it’s a new product, it’ll roll out via our current commercial relationships, because that trust (and legal contracting) took years.”

Ana Milicevic, co-founder of Sparrow Advisers, described the labels as directional distinctions rather than settled product categories. She added, “It’s not all just a collection of black boxes. Then that’s a net new layer in the stack.” The key distinction is whether a platform merely pushes buttons in existing interfaces or makes independent decisions about where budgets go.

Ciarán O’Kane, general partner at First Party Capital, was more skeptical about novelty. In wording expressly cleared for attribution, he characterized UMPs and UBPs as “meta DSPs” that act as workflow layers on top of existing systems by plugging into APIs. He also said many emerging players in the space that use this terminology are revisiting approaches the industry has tried before.

Media-buying teams have long struggled with the fragmentation of conducting ad campaigns using different buying interfaces, datasets, and measurement methods. AI can make the operational work of combining and interpreting those inputs less expensive, but it does not automatically create comparable performance data or independent buying authority.

“The frustration is very, very old. What’s new now is that AI is letting us operationally skip through some hurdles that were previously insurmountable,” Milicevic told Digiday. Tasks that once demanded a substantial analytics stack and specialist labor can increasingly be assisted by repeatable prompts and automated processes.

The commercial question is whether cheaper orchestration creates an entirely new buyer or simply a more convenient way to operate existing platforms. For advertisers, that distinction determines whether the new technology saves money, reallocates money, or merely adds another vendor to pay.

In an Oct. 8 blog post, titled “universal buying platform,” Apostrophe co-founders Brian O’Kelley and Anne Coghlan outlined a broader buying layer intended to connect intent, execution, and measurement across advertising environments, citing the recent launch of Amazon Ads Agent as an example of this trend.

Their argument is a public proposal, not evidence that a single independent platform already performs every part of the workflow at scale — O’Kelley did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment by press time.

Other companies are approaching the problem from different directions, with Olyzon — an entity that used the term “agentic orchestration platform” in its recent CTV launch material — serving as one example.

Jules Minvielle, co-founder and CEO of Olyzon, told Digiday the company does not own inventory and uses agents to coordinate buying through existing platforms, with advertiser-controlled approvals. “A DSP is one place where you can buy. Orchestration is all the work around the buy: building the plan, deciding where each dollar goes, activating across every platform, watching delivery, measuring, and feeding what worked into the next plan,” he wrote in an emailed statement, adding that uneven API access and inconsistent measurement remain substantial obstacles.

Ravi Patel, co-founder and CEO of SWYM.ai, drew a distinction between a common interface and control over the budget. “It can manage the ‘what’ and the ‘how much.’ It can’t manage the ‘which impression.’ That distinction is the whole story,” he explained, adding that campaign setup and pacing across platforms are already possible through APIs. However, impression-level optimization within the major walled gardens remains in their own systems.

Lucas Cridland, CEO of Basin Media, questioned whether agents are changing the underlying buying process. “At the moment, many environments are agentically operating in the same supply chain flow. They’re just doing it with agents buying; they’re not radically changing the way the process works. And that’s where the real edge exists.”

Not necessarily. Many of the new propositions depend on DSPs continuing to execute transactions, access inventory, and optimize within their own environments. The change may be who decides which platform gets the advertiser’s budget in the first place.

Milicevic maintained that “value always accrues to whoever is closest to the decision or outright owns the decision.”

Ergo, if DSPs cannot move upstream into genuine cross-channel decision-making, they risk becoming “dumb pipes,” i.e., a commoditized, essential infrastructure whose underlying value is less visible to buyers.

Incumbent DSPs, however, have established advertiser relationships, bidding technology, data integrations and scale. They could develop their own orchestration capabilities. A new intermediary would need to show that its independence produces better decisions, rather than simply adding another fee, according to several sources consulted for this piece.

Agencies already coordinate campaigns across channels, negotiate buying arrangements and manage the systems needed to execute them. Friedman, a former long-time agency-lead, argued that unified platforms could challenge agency order-management systems while bringing neglected channels, including creator-led media, into a common workflow.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

The biggest obstacle may be the very platforms that independent systems are supposed to coordinate. Google, Meta, and Amazon control their own buying tools, data, and optimization systems. Access through an API does not necessarily provide the granular information or authority required to compare results and independently move budgets.

Trust is also an issue. A unified interface can display reports from multiple platforms without proving that the outcomes are comparable. Attribution windows, campaign objectives, and self-reported measurement differ across channels, meaning an independent buying layer needs a credible basis for deciding that one dollar is better spent in one environment than another.

Friedman was skeptical that interoperability would resolve the underlying problems, and highlighted how Google and Meta are unlikely to make their systems interoperable. He further questioned whether agencies could build the context and routing infrastructure needed to translate employees’ instructions into accurate buying decisions, even if every platform supported the Model Context Protocol. “Interoperability won’t solve anything,” he told Digiday.

Greg MacDonald, founder of Chelsea Strategies, said addressing media buying’s growing complexity could reduce spending on its mechanics and, over time, potentially improve advertiser outcomes. However, he remained cautious about how far unified platforms could go. “I’m skeptical that the walled gardens will give buyers more control than they have today,” he told Digiday, pointing to persistent difficulties with frequency capping across programmatic formats, let alone disconnected marketplaces.

“But you don’t have to solve every problem, and certainly not on day one to be useful,” MacDonald added. He compared the opportunity with programmatic guaranteed buying, which made the insertion-order process more efficient without fundamentally changing direct media buying.

Meanwhile, Milicevic said the more plausible near-term customer may be a multichannel advertiser between the largest global brands and small businesses that largely rely on a single platform. She also stressed the need for independent validation of results, rather than taking each platform’s own reporting at face value.

AI agents could help automate campaign setup, inventory discovery, negotiation, execution, and reporting. That makes agentic advertising relevant to the unified-platform debate, but not synonymous with it: automating tasks is different from making accountable, cross-channel budget decisions.

Unplugged Collective maps seven ways AI could change advertising (see graphic below). Per Weisberg, two are central to the unified buying debate: how agents decide where to spend and how they carry out those decisions across platforms.

Industry initiatives such as AdCP and IAB Tech Lab’s agentic work aim to establish ways for systems to communicate and execute workflows. Technical standards could reduce integration costs, but they cannot by themselves force walled gardens to disclose comparable data or surrender commercial control.

Seasoned industry observers have seen enough buzz phrases come and go to feel confident enough to make tongue-in-cheek observations when a new acronym becomes de rigueur among the industry’s chattering classes.

“I saw ‘UMP’ and I thought: ad network,” quipped Terence Kawaja, CEO, LUMA Partners, a prominent investment bank in the space, speaking on stage at this year’s Prebid Summit – channeling the industry adage, “everything is an ad network.”

Meanwhile, one prominent industry source, who requested anonymity to preserve industry relationships, dismissed the latest acronyms as another serving of “word salad.” However, the barbs, playful or otherwise, raise a palpable industry debate: As AI makes it cheaper to run campaigns across platforms, who gets to decide where the money goes?

The clearest test is whether advertisers entrust meaningful cross-channel allocation to an independent system — and can demonstrate that the decisions improve outcomes. Smaller-scale automation and campaign management do not, on their own, prove that a new market category has emerged.

Milicevic identified three indicators: real advertiser budgets moving under independent allocation, credible measurement outside any single walled garden, and changes in how marketing organizations structure teams. “If it’s truly structural, it’ll show up in the PNL, and if it’s slideware and fluff, then that’ll make its way into award entries and kind of the PR circuit,” she said.

For now, the most useful distinction is between controlling the infrastructure through which ads are purchased and controlling the decision about where the money goes. The latter is the more ambitious promise. Whether anyone can deliver it independently, at scale, and at a cost advertisers will accept remains unresolved.

After 45 years of negotiating, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.

#7422. Maker’s Mark spotlights signature red wax top in visual refresh

The brand evokes its heritage and craftsmanship with a new color palette, website and typography.

Maker’s Mark, a Suntory Global Spirits brand, pulled back the curtain on a new global brand identity this week that updates the brand’s color palette, photography and graphic elements with a more minimal, modern aesthetic that evokes the brand’s heritage. It is live in the U.S., China, Australia and the U.K., with other markets planned throughout 2027, according to a press release.

The new visual identity spotlights the bourbon’s iconic red wax. Custom typography is inspired by the handwriting of Margie Samuels, one of the co-founders of Maker’s Mark, who died in 1985 and was responsible for creating the brand name as well as its original bottle design. The bottle design remains unchanged, per press materials. Tactile elements included in the wax seal, such as wheat stalks and oak rings, were created using materials found at Star Hill Farm, one of the properties owned by the brand and the namesake of its Star Hill Farm Whisky.

"In a world increasingly driven by speed and automation, we still believe some things are worth making slowly, by hand," said Chloe Lloyd-Jones, vice president of global brand marketing at Maker's Mark, in press materials.

The brand last made changes to its visual identity in 2021, but they were not as extensive. The latest refresh was developed in partnership with Turner Duckworth London and is part of the “Perfectly Unreasonable” campaign.

The new design will appear across digital, social and retail channels, along with a new website. The overhaul is supported by in-person experiences in the U.S. and China, along with out-of-home activations in Australia and the U.K.

The refresh comes as the bourbon industry faces significant challenges, including slowing demand and a bloated inventory, which are driving down prices. Tariffs have also taken a toll on the industry, with exports of American whiskey, which includes bourbon, falling 19% in 2025. The industry’s issues are exacerbated by the general trend of Americans drinking less overall. A little over half of Americans said they drink, according to a recent Gallup poll, the lowest it’s been since the organization started measuring the trend in 1939.

Suntory Global Spirits ceased production at its flagship Jim Beam distillery in Clermont, Kentucky at the beginning of 2026. It recently announced it would continue to pause distilling operations until at least the end of next year. Maker’s Mark is distilled at a different location, which remains operational.

“Perfectly Unreasonable” launched in 2025 and is the first campaign for the brand by Carmichael Lynch. In December, Maker’s Mark entered into its first sports league sponsorship and became the first official spirits partner of the women’s basketball league Unrivaled.

Suntory Beverage & Food Limited, which is traded on the Tokyo Stock Exchange, saw its revenue increase 9.8% year-over-year for the nine months ending Sept. 30, 2026, according to financial results.

#7419. Graduate Engineer (remote work) at CloudLinux

#7418. Brands booking creators to make their ads are pushing up $116B global influencer market

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Brand spending on creator marketing is set to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising as a whole over the next five years, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall.

The consultancy estimated that creator spending will rise 12% each year until 2030, versus a 6% increase in ad spending each year between 2025 and 2030. In 2026, the researcher estimates that marketers will spend $116 billion on creator activity.

Driving that growth is ad spend intended to boost or promote creator content to a larger audience — which accounted for 44%, or $52 billion, of global creator marketing spending in 2026 — and brands paying creators to produce advertising assets for use in CTV, display and paid social contexts.

The latter accounted for 12% of global creator spending in 2026, but will rise 16% each year until the end of the decade, making it the fastest-growing segment of the creator economy. Traditional sponsored posts or ambassador relationships accounted for 28% of creator spending.

Brands attempting to reach large audiences on social platforms have begun to look to creators not just as a distribution point, but as a source of creative production that can meet the need for a diverse set of creative assets at a lower price point than traditional agency methods.

Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, for example, recently said that the CPG giant’s social content had to be constantly refreshed to prevent it from going stale. “The lifespan of a video today is four days,” he said during the firm’s recent Capital Markets Day. “Content has to be renovated because after four days you don’t have diminishing levels of return, you have negative marginal return.”

“The reason we see brands engaging with this is just because it’s cheaper,” said Luke Stillman, managing director at Madison & Wall. “Everybody is trying to squeeze down all those non-working expenses as much as possible.”

As creator rosters get larger — Unilever now works with around 500,000 — the need to manage such programs has driven spending on specialist agencies, platforms and talent representation. Spending on creator intermediaries (fees for specialist agencies, platforms, or talent representation) accounted for 8% of global creator spending.

Stillman said he expected to see spending on that slice grow further in coming years. “Identifying the right creators, contracting with all those creators, managing non-delivery and legal issues and payment issues are really complex, and so there’s plenty of space for ancillary services there,” he said.

“We’re seeing an increase on budgets year-on-year from our clients,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo. Ryan estimated client spending on creator marketing had increased 20%-30% in 2026.

Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social, saw an even bigger jump, estimating that client spending on creator activity had risen 50% compared with last year. “I would anticipate that will only continue to grow quite heavily,” she said.

The U.S. market accounted for 43% of creator spending last year, per Madison & Wall estimates, while the U.K. and Germany are the next-largest, at 5% and 4% respectively. The U.S. will see spending grow 15% this year. India was the fastest growing market, at 20%.

Madison & Wall’s estimate for asset-led spending covered several related practices: whitelisting, fees for user-generated content, content licensing, and fees for producing creative assets.

However brands are commissioning it, creator-led production is on the rise. “More of our brand partners are bringing creators in as the production engine for their paid media, not just their organic social,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer at Viral Nation.

While cost is a major factor, as Stillman noted, agency marketers also believe creator-made work can engage audiences more effectively than an ad made for the same environment by a creative director.

“Creator-made assets simply connect best with potential customers on a human level,” said Spiro. “They tend to drive performance better than traditional brand creative, and they can be more measurable for certain objectives than organic creator-only content.”

While creator marketing was once dominated by apparel and beauty brands, healthcare, automotive and finance advertisers now spend on creator activity too.

“Where some sectors like fashion and beauty have been on this for a number of years, and therefore they’re already doing a fantastic job at it… some sectors [were] behind and they’re catching up and are now throwing more at it,” said Ryan.

“An ad can make a claim. A parent can prove it,” said Carmen Graham, CMO at Genexa, a youth-targeted healthcare brand.

“Parent creators can also do what we can’t: show the product in real use, in their home. The messier and less art-directed the medicine cabinet, the more we see ourselves and our customer in the content,” she said.

“Our bet is that this commitment to being parent-first will drive the trust this category requires — especially if you’re switching to a new product to support you in the most dire moments of parenthood.”

Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

#7416. Paid media now plays largest role in brand-funded creator growth: report

While pumping paid dollars into creator content is the biggest piece of the category pie, using creators for production is the fastest growing, Madison & Wall found.

Madison & Wall’s research illustrates how creator marketing is evolving further away from the narrow #sponsored post model — what the advisory and consulting firm calls audience-led spend — into a multifaceted media engine that is core to consumer outreach. The advertiser-funded creator economy is forecast to grow 15% this year to $116 billion and reach $178 billion by 2030. It is on pace to grow at twice the rate of the total ad market as brands try to meet demands around creative content volume, authenticity and audience access.

“[Creators] have become an essential layer of the commercial advertising and media supply chain, serving simultaneously as media channels, creative production houses, commerce drivers, and performance engines,” the report reads.

Putting paid media dollars behind existing creator content that performs well has become the largest aspect of the advertiser-funded creator economy, providing reach and scalability without the TV commercial gloss that many consumers have begun to reject. Reliably achieving organic reach has also become less certain as platform algorithms change. Due to these factors, media-led creator spending is expected to reach $83 billion by 2030.

Marketers are not broadly expanding their budgets to support creator initiatives, but rather siphoning off resources previously allocated to channels like traditional media, digital search, paid social and programmatic. Madison & Wall emphasized that creator partnerships often employ multiple approaches, including combining audience-led and media-led spend, rather than focusing on one bucket. The study did not analyze consumer-funded creator income, meaning subscriptions, tips or other forms of revenue creators receive from their audiences.

While media-led spending is the biggest piece of the creator pie at the moment, asset-led spending, where brands pay creators to produce content, effectively using them as a content studio, is the fastest growing, currently representing 12% of the market. That helps explain the budgets being drawn from traditional creative production and could be a detriment to third-party creative agencies and production shops, which are also contending with the rise of artificial intelligence-generated content.

Those marketing services intermediaries, as well as talent managers and technology platforms, are retaining about 8% of advertiser-funded creator investments on a net basis as part of what Madison & Wall terms infrastructure spend. The rest of the market is made up of a miscellaneous segment that accounts for 7% of spend and encompasses creator engagements such as live events, barter arrangements and name, image and likeness deals.

The second part of Madison & Wall’s study will break down some of the growing pains the creator economy faces as it looks to sustain momentum, including fragmentation, pricing inconsistencies, contractual logjams, measurement shortcomings and questions of ownership over areas like data and intellectual property. That installment will also dive into creator fatigue, a mounting problem for marketers who have valued creators as a more approachable and trustworthy way to catch consumer attention.

#7415. Media Briefing: ChatGPT’s new business hub is an early ‘search console moment’ for publishers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Briefing covers the latest in media trends for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Thursday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Media Briefing looks at what publishers can learn from ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data, how that data fits into their third-party analytics, and what it means for their GEO businesses.

OpenAI’s new Business Hub gives brands and publishers access to something they’ve never had before: a first-party view of how they appear in ChatGPT. But legal teams may need to read the fine print before publishers dive in.

Last week, OpenAI launched a ChatGPT Business Hub, a dashboard for businesses that claim and verify their accounts. It offers data on how their sites appear in ChatGPT, including mentions, profile views, website clicks and the topics driving discovery. For publishers, that offers an early view into activity they’ve largely had to estimate with third-party tools or infer from referral traffic.

“Until now, everything publishers knew about their presence in ChatGPT came from third-party tools running prompts at scale and estimating, or from referral traffic after the fact. This is a very early, very limited Search Console moment for ChatGPT,” said John Shehata, CEO and founder of publisher SEO analytics firm NewzDash.

Publishing execs are mixed on how useful they thought the data would be for their AI visibility and audience development strategies. Of the half dozen large publishers Digiday spoke with for this story, half were in the process of setting up their business accounts.

It’s early days, but one industry exec, who requested anonymity, said legal teams may need to be extra cautious around some of the potential implications of OpenAI’s terms of service.

OpenAI’s terms require businesses to agree not to pursue certain legal claims against the company, including disputes involving content published on their websites before they signed up. That may cause headaches for legal teams at publishers already grappling with how to protect and monetize their content in the AI era, if accessing valuable first-party visibility data comes at the cost of relinquishing certain legal rights — a trade-off that may give some pause before signing up.

When reached for comment, an OpenAI spokesperson said the terms “were not intended to cover this scenario.”

Despite those concerns, some publishers see a potential commercial benefit. Two publishing execs said they were interested in using the data to support their GEO offerings. AI attribution and measurement is a major hurdle for those businesses, but they said insights from brands’ first-party data could help publishers assess the performance of branded content campaigns alongside data from third-party tools.

“These insights potentially provide a really valuable piece of the puzzle that should validate our own success criteria around visibility and citations,” said Carly Steven, Daily Mail director of SEO and editorial ecommerce.

Shehata said the Business Hub data could help publishers demonstrate the value of their GEO offerings to brands, but cautioned that a publisher’s own visibility metrics don’t prove a brand’s campaign delivered results.

“With authorized access to a client’s data, [publishers can] finally prove that recommendations move real platform metrics. That closes the measurement loop missing from every GEO pitch. But a publisher’s own citation count is not proof of a client’s GEO return. A brand campaign still needs evidence at the brand level. The commercial opportunity is selling better evidence and better decisions, not citation counts,” he said.

Another publishing exec was less convinced the data would help their GEO business.

“Since [the hub] is only [tracking] mentions of the business owner, it isn’t so useful to our needs around influencing AI answers on behalf of partner brands. But we are signing up just so we can see if there’s anything there,” the exec said.

For example, if a brand pays for a publisher’s GEO offering, that publisher produces sponsored content articles about that brand’s product, and ChatGPT recommends the product with a citation link to the publisher’s sponsored content, the Business Hub wouldn’t report the mention of the product to the publisher, the exec explained. This means publishers will need to continue to use third-party AI visibility tools to measure those citations.

“At this stage, I see brand mentions in answers akin to billboards. It makes sense for Nike to have a billboard but not [a publisher],” the exec said.

Lily Ray, vp of SEO strategy and research at performance marketing agency Amsive, pointed out that the Business Hub tracks mentions of a business website, rather than its brand name surfacing in ChatGPT.

That offers a “limited view” of what is considered a mention in ChatGPT, though Ray expects the measurement to evolve. It also makes click-through rates appear disproportionately high. On the Business Hub dashboard for her SEO consultancy, Ray recorded four clicks from just 25 mentions. While “they’re very small numbers in absolute terms,” Ray said the first-party data gives her another point of comparison against metrics from third-party tools.

While the dashboard offers an incomplete picture, Shehata said publishers can still use its data to establish a baseline and track changes over time. He recommends three steps  publishers take once they get access to their Business Hub data. The first is to use the data to set a baseline for mentions, clicks and discovery topics so that the publisher can evaluate trends over time.

The second is to use discovery topics, which are “the most valuable data for audience teams,” to steer editorial experiments, Shehata said. These topics show which themes ChatGPT associates with a publisher’s site, where the AI treats the publisher as an authority, and white spaces around topics the publisher wants to own.

“A travel publisher seeing activity around family travel can strengthen those guides with original reporting and watch whether the numbers move,” Shehata said.

Publishers should also separate the metrics in the Business Hub dashboard and their outcomes, he added.

“A citation is a visibility outcome. A visit is an audience outcome. A subscription or sale is a business outcome. Publishers need to measure those separately,” Shehata said. He suggested publishers compare Business Hub trends against their own analytics, such as ChatGPT referrals, newsletter signups and conversions.

“If reported visibility grows while referrals stay flat, that raises a hard question about value the dashboard alone won’t answer,” Shehata said.

The Business Hub analytics have a number of limitations that publishers should consider too,  Shehata noted. The data is aggregated, with no prompts, no per-article breakdown, and no competitor benchmarks, he said.

The lack of prompt data means the analytics lack what third-party prompt tracking can provide – and that’s saying something, considering prompt tracking is “deeply flawed,” said Barry Adams, founder of Polemic Digital, an SEO and audience growth consultancy for news publishers. Digiday previously covered the varying methodologies for gathering prompt data by different AI visibility analytics firms, and how much of it relies on synthetic data or panel data, and the efforts publishers have to go through to weed out the GEO solutions from the snake oil.

“We don’t know the contexts in which our websites are mentioned, except in the vaguest of terms. That makes it very hard to glean anything useful from these reports,” Adams said. However, Adams pointed out that over time, Google added more useful tracking to its Search Console dashboard, and hoped the Business Hub would also continue to evolve.

Even though the data is limited compared to what prompt tracking tools offer, the fact that the data is coming from the AI platform itself makes it “extremely valuable,” Ray said.

“It’s another data point for a very convoluted channel,” she said. “It’s not going to replace any of the tools I’m using.”

“This year, we kind of saw more volume. Smaller spends, but new advertisers. It’s too early to tell why that’s happening. If branded content is kind of going in a different direction because of AI and publishers are getting more media spend? I don’t really know. It’s been two wildly different years, this year versus last year.”

— John Sills, head of sales, North America at The Guardian

#7411. Advertisers suspend TikTok activity in response to Spanish CSAM investigation

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Several advertisers in Spain, including consumer bank Santander, have paused their activity on TikTok due to concerns over videos containing child sexual abuse material (CSAM) circulating on the platform.

The pause comes in response to reports from investigative journalism outlet Maldita.es published last week. Two major advertising clients of another agency holding company have paused TikTok spending, Digiday understands, while energy company Moeve has also paused its TikTok activity according to reports in the Spanish press.

The investigation found that videos that include CSAM, or promoted websites that sold CSAM, ran before or after ads from 21 brands including Ralph Lauren, Banco Santander, Pringles, Samsung, Philips, Nescafé, JD Sports and Apple.

In response to the investigation, Sira Rego, the Spanish youth and children minister, asked the country’s data protection agency to temporarily suspend access to the app. As of yesterday (November 16th), the issue is now being investigated by Spain’s public prosecution office.

Santander, the fourth largest bank in Europe, suspended its advertising on TikTok following the investigation’s release. Publicis Groupe has held the advertiser’s global media and creative account since October 2025. Santander did not respond to a request for comment by time of publication.

“We are aware and the team has been working with clients on the ground,” said an Omnicom spokesperson; they declined to say whether the holdco’s clients had also suspended TikTok activity.

Spokespeople for Publicis Groupe, WPP, Havas and Dentsu declined to comment. Stagwell did not respond to a comment request by time of publication.

TikTok has been attempting to reassure brands that its moderation systems can keep videos depicting abuse off its platform.

According to a note circulated among advertisers and media agencies, the platform said a “cross-functional task force” was responding to the Maldita.es reports. “We remain deeply committed to our platform being a safe and positive experience for teens,” said the note.

“Our automated moderation technology reviews content uploaded to TikTok to identify violations of our Community Guidelines. These systems examine a variety of signals, including keywords, images, captions, and audio. If a potential violation is identified, the automated moderation system will either forward it to our safety teams for further review or remove it automatically if it breaches our policies.”

So far, those measures have not reassured clients previously active on the platform. Laura Camazón, head of digital and innovation at Mediaplus Equmedia, said three of the agency’s clients had paused their activity on TikTok.

“Until we know what’s happening, it’s better to pause,” she told Digiday. Camazón said the agency’s clients will likely suspend paid activity on TikTok until 2027; she declined to name the  brands in question.

In a statement provided to Digiday, a TikTok spokesperson said: “We condemn child sexual abuse and exploitation. Child Sexual Abuse Material is categorically prohibited on our platform, and we deploy advanced detection technologies alongside a dedicated Child Safety Team to aggressively identify and eliminate it.

“Alongside these ongoing actions, as soon as these claims were published, we took further action to identify and remove content, and submit reports to The National Center for Missing & Exploited Children where appropriate. As bad actors continuously adapt their tactics, we work tirelessly to improve our safeguards, and we will continue to actively cooperate with authorities, as we have always done.”

TikTok does not break out ad revenue data for Spain, but its combined advertising revenue for Europe, the U.K., Africa and Latin America totalled $9.2 billion last year. TikTok has around 26 million users in Spain, according to EU filings.

After 45 years of negotiating, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

#7409. How Disney helps brands interact and transact with holiday audiences

The entertainment giant’s holiday ad sales are pacing 20% ahead of the same point last year, with demand across the retail, financial services, CPG and technology sectors.

The 60th annual Country Music Association Awards, which aired last night on ABC and Disney+ and will stream tomorrow on Hulu, showcased Disney’s evolving advertising strategy in action. Chevrolet debuted new custom creative, partnered with musician and Chevy truck owner Zach Top for several in-show moments and put out social-first, behind-the-scenes content.

In addition, Bath & Body Works, a new partner to the awards show, was the first brand to be a presenting sponsor of a live integration on TikTok that captured behind-the-scenes content and was hosted by musician Lauren Alaina and creator Haley Baylee. Financial services company Ally made its CMA Awards debut, activating on in-show, social, streaming and linear channels, including content with personality Kristin Cavallari.

“We're looking at new ways beyond your traditional 30-second ad in order to be able to engage fans as well as drive new revenue opportunities,” said John Campbell, senior vice president for streaming, entertainment and multicultural solutions at Disney.

The CMA Awards arrived with just a week until Thanksgiving. The holiday period is in full swing, with the National Retail Federation forecasting holiday retail sales of more than $1 trillion. With a portfolio that spans film, TV, streaming, theme parks and consumer products, Disney reaches 54% of holiday gift-givers monthly, per the company’s research, providing advertisers with a large audience across channels.

For Disney, advertising bookings in streaming and linear across Halloween, Thanksgiving and the holidays overall are pacing 20% ahead of the same point last year, with demand across categories including retail, financial services, CPG and technology, the company said.

The holidays are now a yearlong concern, with Disney selling its first holiday deal in January and more than 10% of its holiday revenue coming in since Nov. 1. The company also saw 10% year-over-year advertising revenue growth in Q4 of its fiscal year 2026 for a total of $1.69 billion. Across the advertising landscape, holiday ads are starting as early as September, per Nielsen.

“The market is still very active,” said Campbell. “Not only are [brands] starting way earlier to build out these larger programs, but they're also coming in as we get closer to the holiday season, and we're seeing total investments grow, as well.”

Brands flock to Disney for three reasons, Campbell said. The company is a trusted source for storytelling with titles that span generations, from “Home Alone” to “The Nightmare Before Christmas;” it delivers live cultural moments, including holiday TV specials, that serve as shared experiences; and it gives advertisers multiple ways to reach consumers across platforms.

Disney’s multichannel approach relies on a StorySync strategy that works with brands to develop seven key elements: sponsorships, in-show integrations, custom content, social and influencer, commerce, experiential and halo enhancements. The company is working to add new shoppable activations to the StorySync framework that can help drive brand revenue during the holiday period.

“We not only encourage people to participate and interact with these brands, but also give them the levers in order to be able to transact with these brands through our shoppable moments,” Campbell said. “How do we add value for our consumers and our viewers, and how do we bring brands to be able to help us add that value?”

“Dancing with the Stars,” now in its 35th season, continues to be a durable property for Disney, with the competition show seeing an 11% episode-to-episode viewership increase en route to the season finale on Nov. 24, Campbell said. Sephora served as the seasonlong beauty sponsor for the show, partnering on social-first custom content and becoming the first show partner to incorporate Disney’s Shop the Stream solution.

The show will keep the action going with a holiday special on Dec. 1. Ocean Spray will have a sponsored dance, custom content, in-show mentions and lower-third graphics, while Christmas retailer Balsam Hill provided decorations and will appear in lower-third graphics.

“That franchise has expanded now,” Campbell said. “No matter the opportunity, we're seeing brands really want to attach themselves.”

In addition, Kraft Mac and Cheese is partnering with Freeform’s “25 Days of Christmas” as part of a long-term strategic partnership between Kraft Heinz and Disney that kicked off this summer. Capital One will join as the first presenting broadcast sponsor for the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony on Dec. 8, and Disney has secured new and returning sponsors for the “Wonderful World of Disney: Holiday Spectacular” and New Year’s Rockin’ Eve programming.

After the holiday season, Disney Advertising will continue apace, with the college football playoffs and national championship game, the Grammy and Oscar ceremonies and the Super Bowl, together making up what Disney ad chief Rita Ferro has described as the company’s “biggest year ever.” Next month, the company will begin introducing creator content to Disney+ through a partnership with TikTok that was announced earlier this year.

“We're very excited about some new content formats that streaming is going to be able to provide,” Campbell said, citing microdramas as a possibility.

#7408. GTA 6 could be a windfall for creators, but standing out will be tough

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Rockstar Games’ Grand Theft Auto (GTA) 6 spawned its own cottage industry in the creator economy.

Creators have spent months building toward the official launch today with hype videos teasing 24-hour marathons from top streamers like Kai Cenat and Darren Jason “IShowSpeed” Watkins Jr., and others dipping back into GTA 5 to capitalize on the buzz. GTA 5 was the fourth most-watched video game on the platform in the last week, according to Twitch Tracker. Pre-launch GTA 6 views topped 40 billion views on YouTube, according to a spokesperson.

The creator economy looks very different from when GTA 5 launched in 2013. This time, some creators have found ways to access GTA 6 early by switching regions, building anticipation before the official release. The day before launch, on November 18, it broke the all-time launch week streaming record with 7.88 million concurrent views, according to Steam Charts. Over the weekend, video game reviewers, streamers, musicians and athletes will play in the immersive game world and the real city it’s inspired by, Miami, Florida.

“The scale of this is hard to even wrap our heads around,” said Ashray Urs, head of Streamlabs, who said he hasn’t seen this level of interest in getting involved in streaming since the COVID pandemic.

The immense popularity of GTA 6, which earned $1.5 billion in revenue before anyone could even play it, is both a boon and a hurdle for the creator economy. Streaming platforms will be inundated with live feeds. Standing out will be difficult, but crucial. Brand partnerships can pay dividends, but your content will need to meet expectations.

Creators have been preparing for this moment for over a year, and two separate delays have only further complicated things. “Everyone has been thinking about this moment. Are you going to do a marathon? Are you going to do casual streams? Are you going to do video on demand [VOD] on YouTube?” said Reed Duchscher, CEO and founder of management company Night Media. “A lot of our clients are just going to be constantly streaming.”

Cody “Clix” Conrad, a professional Fortnite player and streamer with 8.7 million followers on Twitch, is missing esports tournaments to make GTA 6 content, telling Digiday he and a group of his creator friends are renting a house to stream 24/7 (other streamer cohorts, including Vanoss Gaming, are doing something similar). The budget is “under 50k for the entire week” to create content, which he says is the “most efficient marketing spend of the week.”

Duchscher said Night Media’s top streamers, which include Kai Cenat and Darryl Dwayne “DDG” Granberry Jr., aren’t just video game creators, and GTA isn’t necessarily their biggest event of the year (Cenat’s Streamer University pulls in millions of viewers and big brand partnerships). Still, he’s expecting they’ll have double their typical concurrent viewership. “We’re quite confident Kai’s going to have over 100,000 concurrents on his live stream,” Duchscher said.

But Cenat has a built-in audience. For other creators, standing out may be tougher: Experts expect “tens of thousands of live streamers” to play GTA 6 in the coming days. Some think creators will have to “lean heavily” into short-form GTA 6 content to stand out.

“There’s going to be a million moments that occur while you play the game over the weekend, [and creators will need to be] the most entertaining reaction of playthrough of those moments,” Conrad said.

Others should take advantage of the game’s ties to the real world. “The landmarks that were in Vice City [the fictional city in GTA 6], are probably the areas where you stream from,” Duchscher suggested. “You have to get really creative because the clippable moments are going to matter so much more.”

Another strategy for the coming weeks: Skip GTA 6 altogether. Nevada Carney, head of strategy at Mutiny, the gaming arm of social-first creative agency Saylor, said some of their partners are thinking about “counter programming.”

“Smaller creators who know it’s going to be so flooded with GTA might be more inclined to stream some other games that are two, three, or four on the list,” Carney said.

It’s a crowded creator field, but one that has been preparing for well over a year, planting partnership seeds that will blossom in the next few weeks. There have been some serious setbacks: Grand Theft Auto 6 was originally slated to release last fall, then delayed to this spring, then again to today. Those delays messed with brand budgets and partnership plans.

“We thought this game was going to come out much earlier, and so we’ve had to move these back, which has then affected budget and has moved budget out of the fiscal year into another fiscal year, and we’ve had to then renegotiate,” Duchscher said.

Conrad said he doesn’t have any specific partnerships for his upcoming marathon, but hosted an IRL event in Miami themed around GTA 6 with his sponsor, JBL, the day before launch.

Noah Eichen, executive creative director at Mutiny, said the brands they work with have pushed planned partnerships and activations into late December or early next year to accommodate.

“There’s a lot of allocation of budgets that happens that way, just to kind build around this behemoth, knowing that it’s coming…it’s unavoidable. But I think that everyone’s been planning for it for so long that a lot of those budgets got shored up a long time ago,” Eichen said.

Interestingly, brand safety appears to be less of a concern than in the past, according to Mike Murphy O’Reilly, chief revenue officer at Dexerto. “GTA has always been one of those things for brands that, when you talk about advertising or doing something in gaming […] it’s at the top of that list as an absolute no,” he said. “GTA 6…the moment is big enough they’re willing to break their brand safety rules.”

There’s also the expectation that GTA 6 will have a very long tail. The game is launching without its infamous multiplayer mode, which kept the previous installment alive and thriving for 13 years. That mode, known as GTA Online, spawned role-playing communities of content creators (known as GTA RP) that drew millions of eyes and sponsors.

“Brands are trying to figure out the longevity of the partnerships…if they can’t get into GTA now, they know their brand can get into episodic type content that will be started with RP,” said Carney. “Brands are scrambling at the moment because they don’t know how to use their media spend…that seems like that’s the bigger question: What’s the long term play with GTA 6?”

The next few days will be flooded with GTA 6 content, clips, and brand activations. How that extends into the holidays and beyond into 2027, when Rockstar will release the game on PC and likely add its online component, remains to be seen.

“There couldn’t be more momentum headed into this game launch…I’m a little concerned it is going to have a hard time living up to the hype,” said Duchscher.

Eichen thinks creator creativity may keep the hype train going. “It’s almost impossible to guess what’s going to come out of this because people are so creative…[creators are] going to build new things that we never imagined,” he said.

ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data can help inform publishers’ GEO strategies and complement third-party analytics.

Creators are tapping into their massive content libraries for syndication opportunities that pay dividends.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

#7407. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Health Coverage: Prioritize your well-being with comprehensive health and dental insurance plans.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt. Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

#7405. Mozilla: Staff Software Engineer, OS Integration

The Mozilla Corporation is wholly owned by the non-profit 501(c) Mozilla Foundation. This means we aren’t beholden to any shareholders — only to our mission. Along with thousands of volunteer contributors and collaborators all over the world, Mozillians design, build and distribute open-source software that enables people to enjoy the internet on their terms.

The OS Integration team ensures Firefox works well with the host operating system and environment. We’re a distributed team with expertise in Windows, macOS, and Linux-based operating systems. Our responsibilities include developing fixes and improvements for Firefox related to Windows, macOS, and Linux environments. This might include integration with a new OS feature or fixing an OS-specific user interface bug. As a member of the OS Integration team, you’ll develop fixes, improvements, and new features for Firefox. The role will lean on your Windows expertise, but projects may have a cross-platform component. #7404. How SharkNinja is doubling down on its creator strategy

The home appliance brand introduced two new creator programs, SharkNinja First Fans and SharkNinja Storytellers, to tap into the quickly growing influencer economy.

SharkNinja CEO Mark Barrocas in 2008 told a retailer that his plan was to build the company “one five-star review at a time.” In Barrocas’ retelling, the retailer’s executives dismissed the idea as foolish because they couldn’t imagine who would write a review of the brand’s products. That was before the rise of influencers.

Over the ensuing 28 years, SharkNinja has become a multibillion-dollar global company, which built its brand through a social-forward, ultra-connected strategy that includes partnerships with celebrities including David Beckham, Courteney Cox and Alix Earle, alongside a host of influencers.

SharkNinja is doubling down on that strategy with two new programs designed to give storytellers, both inside and outside of the company, access and opportunities to develop and create even more content.

The programs, SharkNinja First Fans, for company employees, and SharkNinja Storytellers, for general consumers and creators, are designed to help the company continue to grow and manage its expanding list of creators and partners.

“We felt we were underserving this community by not creating a space where we could have more scaled one-to-ones with them, bring them further into the fold in terms of our product board maps, and incentivize participation,” said Michelle Crossan-Matos, chief brand and experience officer at SharkNinja.

Over 200,000 posts about SharkNinja’s varied household products, which include blenders, vacuums and ice-cream makers, have been made this year, garnering more than 19 billion impressions, according to the company. Last year, it recorded more than $6.4 billion in sales.

The secret sauce is the company’s practice of fostering, nurturing and actively supporting those who want to create content around SharkNinja’s products, according to Crossan-Matos.

“We bring creators all the way upstream with our products, and we’re having them react to and test and make content with some of our products before we go to market. It’s always been core for our strategy,” said Crossan-Matos.

The new programs use leaderboards and rewards to ensure the content keeps coming. For outside creators, the rewards, which can include commissions and perks, are tied to growth and activity. The most active creators will get exclusive first looks at upcoming products and even get a voice in shaping them. The company has created tutorials and other materials, including an artificial intelligence-powered app, to help the internal creators develop compelling narratives.

Though not exactly commonplace, employer-supported creator programs are not unheard of either. Starbucks has its Green Apron Creators, IBM has a Buzz Squad, Gap opened up its creator ecosystem to employees, and Dell has compensated its employees for sharing content on their own channels. The scope of SharkNinja’s creator efforts are notable.

“I don't know if anybody is doing it the exact way that we are,” Crossan-Matos said. “I think to have both [consumer and employee programs] launch as quickly as we did and at this scale … Our appetite is to drive this globally.”

The company can support these programs on a global scale because of its broad product base, Crossan-Matos said. SharkNinja creates products across 41 consumer categories and launches an average of 25 new products every year.

“We're very lucky to have so many products for so many different types of communities globally,” Crossan-Matos said. “Creators just fit so seamlessly into our co-creation and our storytelling. We think [these initiatives] are going to propel more people to tell their own stories in a way that is even more relatable because they're everyday people.”

Keeping an eye on metrics will be key to measuring program success.

“We’ll be looking at content performance, just like we do with everything. We will be looking for what posts are going viral, what things are gaining traction,” Crossan-Matos said. “We expect really high numbers as we expand.”

#7402. Inside Nike’s plan to turn creators into brand builders

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Nike’s turnaround plan gives creators a bigger role in building the brand.

That became clear at its investor day earlier this week (November 17), where execs spent plenty of time on getting closer to athletes and stretched the definition of one to take in far more people.

“When we say athlete, we mean athlete in the broadest sense possible. If you have a body, you are an athlete,” CEO Eliott Hill told investors.

That definition puts everyday participants alongside elite competitors. Separately, Nike is looking to bring creators, cultural voices and local communities closer to the brand as part of its growth strategy. Together, these moves are part of “Full Stride Ahead”, the strategy Nike is counting on to get back to sustainable, profitable growth. Doing so rests on three pillars the brand’s top brass call “Epic”, “Connected” and “Fast.”. They cover product innovation, consumer relationships and speed to market. Connected is where creates and athletes come in.

Brand president Amy Montagne said Nike is changing how it spends its marketing dollars, with more weight on “grassroots connections, local creators and relevant cultural voices”. She said the goal is to reach communities through “the right people, in the right places and at the right moments”.

The splashy camopaigns and superstar deals will carry on. Next to them, Nike is building a looser network of local influence, informed by research across more than 10 markets where it quizzed athletes, retailers, creators and tastemakers about how tastes shift from place to place. Local teams will get more say over product creation, assortments and how the brand shows up in each market. The shift is particularly clear in China, where Nike acknowledged that its previous model was too centralized toward its headquarters in Beaverton, Oregon. It is now moving towards more locally driven product creation and giving teams closer to shoppers greater influence over decisions.

That means changes for creators too. Nike wants their help reading an audience and translating product into local culture, a job that starts well before any campaign lives. The company plans to expand its use of creator networks, entertainment partnerships and athlete-led storytelling.

“Putting the onus on the regions is the right way of thinking as long as it goes deep enough,” said James Kirkham, founder of brand consultancy Iconic. “With JD Sports we worked on ‘second cities’ like Liverpool, Rotterdam and Lyon (these are often port cities for various historic cultural hot spot reasons), which tend to get to culture first.”

The trends in Liverpool are very different from those just 30 miles down the road in Manchester, he continued, right down to the shape and fit of some jogging bottoms and a brand that lumps the two together has already lost so knowing that sheer nuance and level of detail and flagging it is key to proper authentic hits.

Nike’s football plans show this is supposed to work. The company built the strategy around a consumer archetype it calls the “Mad Baller” — a football obsessed teenager who plays the game, watches it and dresses the part. Nike is chasing that kid across what it calls pitch, stands and street which ties elite players and performance boots to fan culture, fashion, creators and grassroots football.

Some of that was on display over the summer. At the World Cup, its “Rip the Script” campaign got 5.7 billion content views, according to the investor day presentation. During it, execs also highlighted how a new generation of creators brought its sotires into football culture generating hundreds of millions of views. Nike then spread the story across individual athlete content, creator collabs, product drops, retail experiences and local tournaments.

Sponsored athletes are in a line for a similar rethink. A player’s value to Nike used to rest mostly on performance and visibility during tournaments. Now, the company wants their training, persona;lities and fan relationships to keep the brand in people’s feeds all year. That also opens up collaborations with creators that reach well beyond the athletes own sport.

Nike seems more interested in bringing creators into how the brand gets built than in buying sponsored posts. A running creator may understand amateur runners better than an ad agency does. A local fashion figure in Lagos or Seoul may have a sharper sense of how sport feeds into streetwear in their city than a global marketing team in Beaverton. People like that can spot trends early, shape storytelling and turn passing interest into actual participation.

Plenty of that work will still look like ordinary campaign and content distribution, of course, and most creators won’t become long-term strategic partners. Yet Nike’s growing use of creator networks could become a competitive advantage, particularly when creators have real standing in a sporting or cultural community. For these creators, the opportunity could extend beyond sponsored content into partnerships involving product, communities and intellectual property.

Nike offered another example of this approach through its Studio Fleece launch. The initiative brought together more than 100 athletes, entertainers, creators and stylists, combining product seeding, creative participation and Nike By You experiences. The collection was distributed across more than 6,000 retail outlets. Nike described it as the largest new apparel launch in its history, with the highest proportion of sales made at full price of any fleece launch.

None of this matters if it doesn’t contribute to sales so Nike is focusing on five priority sports: running, training, global football, basketball and outdoor plus sportswear. More often than not those efforts will be focused on 23 countries that Nike expects to deliver about 90% of its growth opportunity between fiscal 2028 and 2031. The goal is around $5 billion in extra revenue by fiscal 2031, compared with fiscal 2027. Nike expeccts about $4 billion of that to come from the five sports and about one billion to come from sportswear.

Running was the proof point the investor day presentations kept coming back to. The category has delivered nine consecutive quarters of growth from the first quarter of its fiscal year in 2025 through to the first for 2027, including six straight quarters of double-digit growth. That’s around £1 billion revenue for the business.

Nike’s Montagne described the wider goal as building “a repeatable engine to create demand,” and that’s the phrase investors are likely to hold the company to. Indeed, Nike wants a model it can roll out across sports and markets deliberately so it isn’t waiting for the occasional cultural moment to land.

A bigger role in a brand’s strategy raises new questions for creators, athletes and the people who represent them. A creator who helps a brand understand a community and build demand there might reasonably look for something beyond a campaign fee. An athlete who helps build a content platform or a product line could push to share in the long-term value they help create. And as brand-run realtor networks get more sophisticated, ownership of the audience, the content and the IP becomes a bigger part of any negotiation.

Investor Days are always about a company’s growth plans so no surprise that compensation models weren’t on the agenda. Those conversations are more likely to happen deal by deal, and in turn create another boon for the cottage industry of agencies, talent managers and creator businesses that have emerged around the creator economy to put together partnerships that account for the wider value creators and athletes bring.

“If I were managing a creator right now then I’d tell them to play up everything specific about themselves: their expertise, their obsessions, their accent, their postcode. Get into the nerdiest detail you’ve got and bring every bit of it to life,” said Iconic’s Kirkham “The deals Nike is describing will go to deep experts with a real local standing and a million views on last decade’s platforms won’t get anyone really bothered or doing the marketing for you any more, you need to go beautifully deep and nuanced.”

Nike has the scale, product and the distribution. The cultural pull and local knowledge it wants increasingly sit outside the company, with creators and athletes it’s now trying to bring closer to the centre of its business.

Why the Daily Mail’s ad sales team is turning talent agent.

The company is increasingly leveraging partnerships with major sports leagues, college athletics and high-profile athletic events.

OpenAI, Google, Amazon and Walmart are betting on agent-led holiday shopping. Digiday charts how far it still has to go.
